DDOG — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
business-model
  • Datadog sells subscriptions to one multi-tenant SaaS observability platform, billed on metered usage. There is no perpetual license, no hardware, and professional-services revenue is stated as immaterial (10-K FY2025, Item 7).
  • Revenue is disaggregated geographically and in no other way: North America was 71.0% of FY2025 revenue, the United States alone 67.7%, International 29.0% (10-K FY2025, Note 15).
  • The host is the anchor unit and most other products are sold as a per-host uplift on the same installed agent, which is why attaching a product requires no re-instrumentation.
  • The pricing page publishes explicit volume-discount gates at 500+ hosts per month, 3B+ log events per month and 1.2B+ Cloud SIEM events per month [web]. Since 91% of ARR comes from customers spending $100,000 or more (10-Q 2026-Q2), the discounted tier is the company rather than the exception.
  • Every 10-K describes three ASC 606 shapes in identical language: a committed amount of usage apportioned ratably; a committed amount delivered as used (drawdown), recognised as consumed; and a monthly usage subscription, recognised as consumed. Overage above any commitment is recognised as incurred.
  • Thirty-four customers above $10m of ARR at 2025-12-31, against total ARR above $4bn, is at least $340m of ARR — more than 8% — held by 0.1% of customers, and the AI-native cohort's eight $10m+ accounts at Q2 2026 belong to that group.
  • The 2-product band saturated four years ago and has added two points in three years. Every deeper band is accelerating: the 6+ band gained 8 points in the year to June 2026 against 4 points a year in 2023 and 2024, and the 10+ band nearly doubled from 7% to 13%.
  • The FY2025 cost-of-revenue increase of $171.4m was 87.4% third-party cloud infrastructure hosting and software ($149.8m) and 10.6% personnel ($18.2m). In 1H 2026 it was 84.3% hosting ($106.0m of $125.7m). The marginal cost of serving a marginal dollar is almost entirely a payment to AWS, Azure or GCP.
  • Over six years the business converted 25 cents of each incremental revenue dollar into non-GAAP operating profit, with essentially zero GAAP leverage because stock compensation grew as fast as revenue.
  • Management's long-term target of "25% plus" restates the realized six-year incremental rate rather than expanding it.
capital-allocation
  • In FY2023 and FY2025 the entire GAAP profit came from the securities portfolio and more, because the operating business lost money on a GAAP basis in both years. In the most recent quarter 89% of pre-tax income still came from interest.
capitalization
  • Stock compensation was 19.6% of revenue in Q2 2026 against 21.8% a year earlier and 21.9% for FY2025, the first sustained leverage in the company's history and the first sub-20% reading since 2021.
competitive-advantage
  • Datadog holds a real competitive advantage that earns returns above its cost of capital today and is defensible for now, with erosion already visible at the edges. It is not structural in the sense that a payments network or a search franchise is structural, and the two facts that matter most are pricing and the behaviour of its largest account.
  • Multi-product attach compounds, with 85% of customers on two or more products and 13% on ten or more at 2026-06-30 against 83% and 7% a year earlier, and customers on all three pillars spend more than 15x the rest.
  • Datadog's stock-comp-excluded operating margin has been flat at 18-23% for four years while revenue doubled: 18.2% in FY2022, 21.1%, 23.3%, 20.6% in FY2025 and 20.1% in Q2 2026. Dynatrace, growing at half the rate on a smaller base, expanded the same measure from 19.5% to 27.0% over the identical span, and ServiceNow from 24.2% to 28.5%. A business with scale economics shows the unit-cost gap widening with volume; Datadog's does not.
  • Gross margin has now turned against it: 80.8% in FY2024, 80.0% in FY2025 and 78.6% GAAP in Q2 2026, with the 10-Q naming third-party cloud hosting as the cause and quantifying it at $106.0m of the $125.7m first-half cost-of-revenue increase.
  • Realized price for the largest accounts is declining and blended price holds only because new small customers enter at list. Datadog can hold price. It cannot raise it.
  • Under consumption pricing, switching costs protect the relationship rather than the revenue, and that is the structural gap against a network where the fee travels with a transaction the customer cannot suppress.
  • Diffing Item 1 across FY2019 through FY2025, the twelve competitive factors are verbatim identical in all seven filings and the named-competitor list changed once in substance, when Splunk became Cisco after the 2024 acquisition. No 10-K names Grafana Labs, Chronosphere or OpenTelemetry, while all three are among the most-cited competitive topics in analyst Q&A and two of them are Gartner Magic Quadrant Leaders.
  • A tagged data model that makes a trace, a log line, a session replay, a container metric and a security signal join on the same keys is hard to build and harder to retrofit. This is the layer the 1,000 integrations serve and the layer that produces the 15x spend multiple.
  • AWS, Azure and GCP are simultaneously Datadog's landlords (the largest cost-of-revenue line, with $1.4bn of non-cancelable purchase commitments), its named competitors for cloud monitoring, and its growing distribution channel, where the 10-K says marketplace sales compress margin and reduce direct customer relationships. Datadog cannot vertically integrate: building datacenters would convert a 4%-of-revenue capital-intensity business into a 15-20% one and destroy the free-cash-flow profile.
  • Datadog closed most of the margin gap to Dynatrace between 2021 and 2023 and has lost it again since, while the growth gap is intact and widened again in the most recent quarter. That is what a business trading margin for share looks like, which is sensible if the market is unpenetrated and dangerous if it is not.
  • $1,548m is 3.3x Dynatrace and 3.4x Elastic, matching the claim, and 4% of Microsoft's. Where it does hold is the rate: 45.2% of revenue sustained for six years, compounded against 41.5% five-year revenue growth, widens the absolute gap against the pure-plays every year.
  • Atlassian's advantage is seat-based and organisational, so its revenue does not decline when a customer optimises. ServiceNow sells to a central IT budget owner on multi-year committed contracts, which is why its stock-comp-excluded operating margin expanded 4.3 points over four years while Datadog's did not. Committed-seat pricing is why ServiceNow expanded and Datadog did not, and it is what keeps Datadog below the top tier.
competitive-landscape
  • At $3,967m of trailing-twelve-month revenue growing 31.5% (Bloomberg, period end 2026-06-30) it is 1.9x Dynatrace, 2.3x Elastic's entire business across three solutions, and 3.7x Cisco's whole disclosed Observability product category.
  • It is not the leader everywhere: in security it is a sub-scale challenger at roughly $107m of ARR against Palo Alto Networks at $10,606m of trailing revenue. In the parts of the market held by hyperscaler-native tooling and legacy on-premise systems management — about 63% of the market Datadog itself points to — it barely competes.
  • Top three is 26.3%, top five 32.3%, and the Herfindahl index from the nine named players is 302; even assuming the unallocated 63.2% sits with only twenty equal-sized players, the index reaches roughly 500. On any standard threshold that is an unconcentrated market with no price-setter and a leader holding 14%.
  • Datadog holds the premium position by admission, and a premium position inside a fragmented, price-competitive structure means gross margin is defended by selling the customer more products rather than by pricing power on any single product.
  • In February 2024 the CFO put the observability market at "$51 billion… growing 11%" while the CEO said "our market share is still only in the single digits." In February 2026 the CFO put it at "a $30 billion-plus market", the deck showed "$28B in 2026E", and the CEO said share was "in the mid-teens."
  • Over the comparable window Datadog added 5,400 net customers, from 27,300 at Dec-2023 to 32,700 at Dec-2025, while its own net retention went mid-110%'s → high-110%'s → about 120%. Datadog compounds on two mechanisms; Dynatrace on one.
  • A scale check keeps the takeout campaign in proportion: "tens of millions" of new revenue across nearly 100 deals is roughly $20-80m against a log line that added at least $250m of ARR in 2025, so legacy takeout is a real but minority contributor and most log growth is consumption expansion inside existing accounts.
  • announced 2025-11-19 and closed 2026-01-29, at roughly 21x an ARR of over $160m growing triple digits. It converts a point product into a line inside a $10.6bn-revenue vendor with an enterprise security channel Datadog does not have.
  • Implied ARR per customer of roughly $57k against Datadog's $118k company-wide and about $803k in its $100k+ cohort at 2025-12-31 shows the competitive axis: Grafana takes the low-ARPU end and the self-operating engineering teams. Named Grafana customers include Anthropic, NVIDIA, Microsoft and Salesforce, squarely inside Datadog's AI-native target cohort.
  • Cisco's own 10-Q says Observability grew 3% in the nine months to 2026-04-25 and attributes even that to ThousandEyes offset by a decline in Splunk. The full-year figure is stale by 23 points and directionally misleading.
expectations
  • The non-GAAP operating margin was 25% in 2024, 22% in 2025, and the FY2026 guide implies 22.9%, a second consecutive year at or below 23%. The target has been reaffirmed twice without a date and without a path, while the underlying spend targets moved up: the IPO-era research goal of 22-26% of revenue is now described as "around 30%".
  • Management's stated line-item targets (R&D ~30%, S&M mid-20s, G&A ~5%) sum to about 60% of revenue, which against an 80% gross margin caps the non-GAAP operating margin near 20%, below what the company is currently guiding.
  • The only standing long-term financial commitment is a single undated margin number, and the disclosure that set the growth expectation — 130%+ net retention sustained over 16 quarters, published in the 2021 deck — is gone, replaced by a band.
  • Where management and the street diverge is gross margin: the company gives no gross-margin guidance at all, and consensus assumes the decline reverses 70 basis points within two quarters and never resumes.
secular-trends
  • A cohort contributing a flat number of growth points while the company grows 30%+ is a cohort whose own growth rate is compressing hard.
  • The end market does not grow at anything close to Datadog's rate, so every year of 25-30% growth requires another two to three points of share from a base already near 16%.
  • Datadog outgrew the two closest listed comparables by roughly 17 points a year for five straight years: five-year calendar CAGRs of 41.5% against Dynatrace's 24.1% and Elastic's 24.8%.
  • The ultimate driver accelerated 17 points from FY2025 to Q2 2026; Datadog accelerated 7.9 points.
  • Added ARR by type ran roughly 26% new customers, 57% expansion of existing products and 17% cross-sell of new products in 2025, against 44%, 37% and 19% in 2023
  • its units are hosts, containers, GPUs, sessions, tests and log volume, all of which scale with workload rather than with developer headcount, so the "AI shrinks engineering teams" thesis does not touch its pricing unit directly.
technology
  • What differentiates is the combination of one consistently tagged data model across 26 products, a research-and-development budget applied to that one substrate, and the operating leverage that lets a new product reuse the existing ingest, storage, query, alerting and billing layers.
  • The company-wide "mid- to high 90s" is a blend of an 87% single-product business and a 99% ten-product business. That is the sharpest disclosed evidence for the switching-cost argument, and it also means the company-level figure will drift up mechanically as product count rises.
  • It graduated CNCF on 2026-05-21 with over 12,000 contributors from over 2,800 companies and the second-highest project velocity of more than 240 cloud-native projects, behind only Kubernetes.
  • storage, indexing, query language, correlation across signal types, alerting, dashboards, workflow, the AI layer, or the commercial product surface. A customer who moves to OTel instrumentation still needs a backend, and the backend is where all Datadog revenue sits.
  • With the OpenTelemetry SDK alone — whether routed through the Datadog Agent's embedded collector, the open-source Collector, or direct OTLP ingest — five Datadog products are unsupported: App and API Protection, Continuous Profiler, Data Jobs Monitoring, Real User Monitoring and Source Code Integration
  • Over the years OpenTelemetry went from negligible adoption to majority use for metrics, Datadog's company gross revenue retention rose from "mid-90s" at the 2021 investor day to "97% plus" at the 2026 investor day, and net retention went from mid-110%'s at 2023-12-31 to low-120%'s at 2026-06-30.
  • What is hard is doing it while keeping logs in the same tag namespace as metrics, traces and security signals, so a Flex Log query joins to a trace. Flex Logs is a price cut defended by what the log sits next to.
  • By August 2026 Datadog had moved Bits onto a unified AI Credits model at $500 per 500 credits a month billed annually, with a Bits Investigation estimated at about 6.5 credits — roughly $6.50
  • If Datadog is the best context source for agents, the MCP server deepens the data-plane position. If the agent becomes the workflow, Datadog is demoted toward a priced data feed behind somebody else's interface, and the correlated view customers pay a premium for matters less.
value-chain
  • Datadog's FY2025 hosting increment of $149.8m is 4x Dynatrace's and 7x Elastic's on a revenue base 1.7x and 2.0x theirs. Datadog is absorbing disproportionately more hosting-cost growth per revenue dollar than either pure-play peer, and only Dynatrace's subscription line moves in the same direction.
  • The ceiling arithmetic is 15% of ARR at the worst 3.0% rate, or 0.45 points of gross margin; at realistic rates and assuming only part of channel-influenced ARR actually transacts on a marketplace, the drag is 0.1-0.3 points. Non-GAAP gross margin declined 380 basis points from its Q4 2023 peak of 83.4% to 79.6% in Q2 2026. The channel explains at most one eighth of that.
  • Both layers are absorbing the same AI-era infrastructure cost inflation at once: Microsoft's Intelligent Cloud gross margin declined 420 basis points in the window Datadog's declined 180, explicitly "driven by the continued investments in AI infrastructure", and AWS's operating margin declined from 37.0% to 35.4%. If the hyperscalers had gained pricing power over Datadog their own margins would be expanding.
  • The commitment is written to roughly 3.5x the then-current annual hosting run-rate and allowed to amortise toward 1.5x before renewal. The 2024 renewal roughly tripled the dollar commitment while the run-rate rose about 25%, which is how volume buys a rate; management said as much in the quarter it would have been negotiated.
  • BYOC shifts storage and retrieval cost from Datadog's cost of revenue to the customer's own cloud account, raising Datadog's gross margin on those workloads and lowering the effective price the customer pays Datadog. It reduces Datadog's hosting bill without reducing the hyperscalers' total take from the chain.
  • Half the Fortune 500 have a Datadog account and the typical one spends less than a single senior engineer costs, so Datadog has a departmental foothold in most of the Fortune 500 rather than an enterprise standard.
  • A fixed dollar per host on a growing host is a declining share of the customer's compute dollar, which is why attach is necessary.

Datadog sells subscriptions to one multi-tenant SaaS observability platform, billed on metered usage. There is no perpetual license, no hardware, and professional-services revenue is stated as immaterial (10-K FY2025, Item 7).

business-explainer · business-model · ↩ back to dossier

Company

filingsupports ✦ best
We generate revenue from the sale of subscriptions to customers using our cloud-based platform. The terms of our subscription agreements are primarily monthly or annual. Customers also have the option to purchase additional products, such as additional containers to monitor, custom metrics packages, anomaly detection and app analytics. Professional services are generally not required for the implementation of our products and revenue from such services has been immaterial to date.
— Datadog 10-K for fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-18
filingsupports
We have grown rapidly in recent periods, with revenues for the fiscal years ended December 31, 2025, 2024 and 2023 of $3,427.2 million, $2,684.3 million, and $2,128.4 million, respectively, representing year-over-year growth of 28% from the fiscal year ended December 31, 2024 to the fiscal year ended December 31, 2025 and 26% from the fiscal year ended December 31, 2023 to the fiscal year ended December 31, 2024. Substantially all of our revenue is from subscription software sales.
— Datadog 10-K for fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-18
filingsupports
Our cloud based multi-tenant SaaS platform allows for real-time ingestion, and analysis of massive amounts of data, without our customers needing to worry about the provisioning, sizing and capacity of their monitoring platform.
— Datadog 10-K for fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-18
filingqualifies
In the case of a subscription for a committed contractual amount of usage that is delivered as used, a monthly subscription based on usage, or usage in excess of a ratable subscription, we recognize revenue as the product is used, which may lead to fluctuations in our revenue and results of operations.
— Datadog 10-Q for the quarter ended June 30, 2026, 2026-08-06
presentationsupports
We designed the products, so that it was easy to download. No professional services. The way we priced it was on a per host basis, so it could be used by everybody and that promoted early adoption. And since that time, we've expanded the product by adding on other functionalities, such as an APM and logs.
— David M. Obstler, Chief Financial Officer, Datadog presents at William Blair 40th Annual Growth Stock Virtual Conference, 2020-06-09
presentationqualifies
We have a combination of different models, subscriptions with overage. We have drawdowns, et cetera. I would say that about -- what we said so far is about 75% of our business is based on either subscriptions or usage around subscriptions and then about 25% -- 20%, 25% is usage over contract over sort of the contracted amount or month-to-month. That's -- it's been roughly stable. And so there hasn't been really much change in that metric.
— David M. Obstler, Chief Financial Officer, Datadog presents at William Blair & Company 41st Annual Growth Stock Conference, 2021-06-03
presentationsupports
We're not in the same situation than most other software companies that we don't charge per seat. We charge per usage. And our usage is typically related to some other fundamental usage our customers have, such as their usage of infrastructure or network or storage or something else.
— Unknown Executive (Olivier Pomel, Co-Founder and CEO, by context), Datadog Analyst & Investor Day 2026, 2026-02-12

Peer: Dynatrace

filingcontradicts
The Dynatrace Platform Subscription (“DPS”) licensing model provides customers with a flexible, scalable, and transparent subscription for the modern cloud. Under the DPS licensing model, a customer makes a minimum annual spend commitment at the platform level and then consumes that commitment based on actual usage and a straightforward rate card. Any platform capability can be used in any quantity at any time based on the customer’s evolving needs.
— Dynatrace 10-K for fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-20

filingqualifies
If a customer consumes more than their minimum annual spend commitment, they are not charged a penalty-style overage and they can continue to use the platform on an on-demand basis, billed monthly at the same rates as pre-paid consumption. — Dynatrace 10-K for fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-20

Every 10-K describes three ASC 606 shapes in identical language: a committed amount of usage apportioned ratably; a committed amount delivered as used (drawdown), recognised as consumed; and a monthly usage subscription, recognised as consumed. Overage above any commitment is recognised as incurred.

filingsupports ✦ best
The Company generates revenue from the sale of subscriptions to customers using its cloud-based platform. The terms of the Company’s subscription agreements are primarily monthly, annual or multi-year. The Company’s customers can enter into (1) a subscription agreement for a committed contractual amount of usage that is apportioned ratably on a monthly basis over the term of the subscription period, (2) a subscription agreement for a committed contractual amount of usage that is delivered as used, or (3) a monthly subscription based on usage. The Company typically bills customers on an annual or multi-year subscription in advance, with any usage in excess of the committed contracted amount billed monthly in arrears.
— Datadog 10-K for fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-18
filingsupports
For committed contractual amounts of usage, revenue is recognized ratably over the term of the subscription agreement generally beginning on the date that the platform is made available to a customer. For committed contractual amount of usage that is delivered as used, a monthly subscription based on usage, or usage in excess of a ratable subscription, the Company recognizes revenue as the product is used. Subscription revenue excludes sales and other indirect taxes.
— Datadog 10-K for fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-18
filingsupports
To the extent that our customers’ usage exceeds the committed contracted amounts under their subscriptions, either on a monthly basis in the case of a ratable subscription or once the entire commitment is used in the case of a delivered-as-used subscription, they are charged for their incremental usage. Usage is measured on a per-unit basis, with the unit of measure differing for each product, based on the unit that, in working with customers and design partners, best indicates the value we deliver.
— Datadog 10-Q for the quarter ended June 30, 2026, 2026-08-06

This slide really shows how our economic model works. It's very, very similar to the cloud providers, which essentially all of our customers are familiar with. We have volume-based pricing. But as our customers grow with us, we bend the curve of cost for them. So they continue to get more and more value, but more and more leverage for that value as their volumes grow. We give them discounts for the amount of commitment that they make to us. We give them discounts for using multiple products, multiple combination of products. We give them discounts for term length. So the more a customer grows with us, the more we're going to bend that curve over time.
— Adam Blitzer, Chief Operating Officer, Datadog, Inc. Analyst/Investor Day, 2026-02-12

How do we keep track of all of this? We think it's tagging, the labeling of everything that's going on with characteristics, you can search for and analyze. In the past, when there was physical hardware, an application related to each, and the timing was often done by labeling each thing with a unique identifier, for instance, a machine name. But looking at each thing individually does not work in the cloud. Unified tagging is a key insight into making this work. ... All the competitors will say they have tagging, but we believe no one else does unify tagging as well and as deeply as we do. In 2009, we came up with the idea that was a key insight that made it work. Everything is centered around tagging from a technical standpoint. ... We have architected the whole back end around tag so that they are completely unified. And that unique correlation happens in a way that is both automatic and invisible for customers.

Atlassian's advantage is seat-based and organisational, so its revenue does not decline when a customer optimises. ServiceNow sells to a central IT budget owner on multi-year committed contracts, which is why its stock-comp-excluded operating margin expanded 4.3 points over four years while Datadog's did not. Committed-seat pricing is why ServiceNow expanded and Datadog did not, and it is what keeps Datadog below the top tier.

And remember, it's -- we are a usage-based model, right? So if you are looking at a seat-based company, you'd have a perfectly smooth line into Infinity. In our case, we -- customers use more or less of our products. Usually, they use more, as you can see, over time. ... And we do see optimization on a regular basis. Sometimes we actually tell customers to optimize, hey, it doesn't look healthy. If you do something about in addition, we can help. We give them discounts for using multiple products, multiple combination of products. We give them discounts for term length. ... filingqualifies
Under the DPS licensing model, a customer makes a minimum annual spend commitment at the platform level and then consumes that commitment based on actual usage and a straightforward rate card. Any platform capability can be used in any quantity at any time based on the customer's evolving needs. If a customer consumes more than their minimum annual spend commitment, they are not charged a penalty-style overage and they can continue to use the platform on an on-demand basis, billed monthly at the same rates as pre-paid consumption.
— Dynatrace, Inc. Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-03-31

Dollar-based gross retention rate is calculated by first calculating the point-in-time gross retention as the previous year ARR minus ARR attrition over the last 12 months, divided by the previous year ARR. The ARR attrition for each month is calculated by identifying any customer that has changed their account type to a "free tier," requested a downgrade through customer support or sent a formal termination notice to us during that month, and aggregating the dollars of ARR generated by each such customer in the prior month.

Peer: Amazon Web Services

websupports ✦ best
Public offer listing fees: Listing fees for software and data public offers are determined by the deployment method: Software-as-a-service (SaaS) - 3%; Server (Amazon Machine Image (AMI), container, and machine learning) - 20%; AWS Data Exchange - 3%. Private offer listing fees: Listing fees for private offers are determined by the total contract value and whether the private offer is renewed from a previous private offer or a previous agreement outside of AWS Marketplace: Less than $1M - 3%; Between $1M and less than $10M - 2%; Equal to or greater than $10M - 1.5%; All renewals - 1.5%.
— Understanding listing fees for AWS Marketplace sellers - AWS Marketplace Seller Guide (accessed 2026-08-10), 2026-08-10
webqualifies
CPPO products have a 0.5% uplift on the listing fee, regardless of the offer type or deployment method. For example, if the product is a SaaS private offer with a total contract value of less than USD $1M, the listing fee would be 3.5%. AWS Marketplace deducts this listing fee from the amount disbursed to the ISV. The listing fee is based on the discounted price offered by the ISV to the channel partner.
— Understanding listing fees for AWS Marketplace sellers - AWS Marketplace Seller Guide (accessed 2026-08-10), 2026-08-10

It reduces Datadog's hosting bill without reducing the hyperscalers' total take from the chain.
